### What RD8 will read: the la and the dates in KL

KL is the one book of his on BDRC with open scans and a full etext. Its printed page is the image number minus 22 (checked on the printed folios of img. 221, 223, 224 and 278). Read: the etext (BDRC's computer input) as the base; Yigdzin-1 over img. 220–226 and 277–279 as a witness (through the slot lock); the tables, the footnote and the lines of pp. 201 and 256 on the scan, by eye, enlarged 2–5×; the classical lines of p. 199 and the dates of p. 255 where the etext and Yigdzin-1 agree.

**Ch. 10, *The la and the la channel* (བླ་དང་བླ་རྩའི་སྐོར, bla dang bla rtsa'i skor), pp. 198–204, img. 220–226.** The la (བླ, bla), "the life-spirit", and its channel (བླ་རྩ, bla rtsa). The chapter opens by calling the la's course and places a part of Tibetan medicine that comes from the reckoning (རྩིས, rtsis), the joining of medicine and reckoning being a feature of Tibetan medicine of its own. It quotes, from the Root Tantra (p. 199, img. 221; the etext and Yigdzin-1 agree):

> རླུང་ནད་དབྱར་དུས་དགོང་དང་ཐོ་རངས་ལྡང་། མཁྲིས་པ་སྟོན་དུས་ཉིན་དགོང་མཚན་དགོང་ལྡང་། བད་ཀན་དཔྱིད་དུས་སྲོད་དང་སྔ་དྲོ་ལྡང་

"Wind illness rises in summer, in the evening and at dawn; bile in autumn, at midday and at midnight; phlegm in spring, at dusk and in the morning."

and from the Explanatory Tantra (བཤད་རྒྱུད, bshad rgyud), «རྩ་གཅིག་བླ་དང་འདྲ་སྟེ་འཁྱམ་པ་ཡིན།», "one channel is like the la: it wanders", calling the la channel one of the three channels the medical texts count as important. The footnote to the first quotation (p. 199, read on the scan): «གསོ་རིག་རྒྱུད་བཞི་ལྕགས་པར་མའི་རྩ་རྒྱུད་གནས་ལུགས་ནད་གཞི་ལེའུ་ཤོག་ངོས༡༠ཐ༡༡ལ་གསལ།», "in the Iron-block print (ལྕགས་པར་མ, lcags par ma) of the Four Tantras, Root Tantra, chapter on the basis of illness, page 10, line 11" (the etext has «ཤོག་ངོས༡», page 1; the scan has ༡༠). In his own words (pp. 199–201, summarised): the la is a refined essence of the elements and of the drops, moving day by day; parts of it can leave the body through imbalance, chiefly by the ring fingers and ring toes, or be drawn off or stolen, and is called back by rites; a blow, pressure, bloodletting or moxa on the la's place can harm the body and even the life (ཚེ་སྲོག, tshe srog), so these are avoided there in massage; its benefits and the signs of its loss (low spirits, unclear memory, frights in dreams). Then the facts of the reckoning (p. 201): the la moves through the body with the turning of the mansions or the course of the planets, a mantra syllable showing at each place; it passes through about three places a day, so the place of the day and those of the day before and after are watched; and «ཟླ་བ་རེ་ལ་རྒྱུ་བ་གཅིག་དང་ཉི་མ་རེ་ལ་རྒྱུ་བ་གཅིག་བཅས་རིགས་གཉིས་ཡོད་པ», there are two kinds, a course over each month and a course over each day.

The course over the month, by the date (ཚེས་གྲངས, tshes grangs), with the la's place and its syllable (pp. 201–202, img. 223–224, the table read on the scan; the syllables stand only beside dates 1–15 and 30):

| Date | The la's place | Syllable | Date | The la's place |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | the big toe (རྐང་པའི་མཐེ་བོང, rkang pa'i mthe bong) | ཨ (a) | 16 | the nape and the jaw (ལྟག་པ་ཟ་འགྲམ, ltag pa za 'gram) |
| 2 | the instep joint (བོལ་ཚིགས, bol tshigs) | ཨ | 17 | the neck (སྐེ་མཇིང, ske mjing) |
| 3 | the calf (བྱིན་ཉྭ, byin nywa) | ཨི (i) | 18 | the palm (ལག་མཐིལ, lag mthil) |
| 4 | the knee (པུས་མོ, pus mo) | ཨི | 19 | the forearm (ལག་ངར, lag ngar) |
| 5 | the hollow of the knee (སྒྱིད་ཁུག, sgyid khug) | ཨུ (u) | 20 | the shoulder blade (དཔུང་སོག, dpung sog) |
| 6 | the fold of the thigh (བརླ་སུལ, brla sul) | ཨུ | 21 | the side of the ribs (རྩིབ་ལོགས, rtsib logs) |
| 7 | the hip joint (དཔྱི་ཚིགས, dpyi tshigs) | ཨེ (e) | 22 | above the kidneys (མཁལ་སྟེང, mkhal steng) |
| 8 | above the kidneys | ཨེ | 23 | the hip joint |
| 9 | the side of the ribs | རི (ri) | 24 | the fold of the thigh |
| 10 | the shoulder blade | རི | 25 | the hollow of the knee (སྒྱིད་ཁུང, sgyid khung, so spelled here) |
| 11 | the forearm | ལི (li) | 26 | above the knee (པུས་སྟེང, pus steng) |
| 12 | the palm | ལི | 27 | the calf |
| 13 | the throat (སྐེ་མགུལ, ske mgul) | ཨོ (o) | 28 | the ankle joint (ལོང་ཚིགས, long tshigs) |
| 14 | behind the ear and the jaw (རྣ་ལྟག་ཟ་འགྲམ, rna ltag za 'gram) | ཨོ | 29 | the instep (རྐང་བོལ, rkang bol) |
| 15 | the crown of the head, the whole body (སྤྱི་གཙུག་ལུས་སྤྱི, spyi gtsug lus spyi) | ཨཾ (aM) | 30 | the sole of the foot, the whole body (རྐང་མཐིལ། ལུས་སྤྱི, rkang mthil, lus spyi), ཨཾ |

Over dates 1–15 the la rises from the big toe to the crown; over 16–30 it comes down again. BDRC's etext scrambles this table; read it from the scan.

The course over the day, by the time of day (pp. 202–203, img. 224–225, read on the scan): at daybreak (ནམ་ལངས, nam langs) in the «ལྒང་བ» (lgang ba, as printed; below); at sunrise (ཉི་ཤར, nyi shar) in the neck (མཇིང་བ, mjing ba); when the sun warms (ཉི་དྲོས, nyi dros) in the lips and the nape; at midday (ཉིན་ཕྱེད, nyin phyed) in the ribs of the chest; when midday turns (ཕྱེད་ཡོལ, phyed yol) in the belly (ལྟོ་བ, lto ba); when the sun is past (ཉི་ཐལ, nyi thal) at the heart (སྙིང་ག, snying ga); at dusk (མུན་རུབ, mun rub) in the small of the back; from evening to daybreak (སྲོད་ནས་ནམ་ལངས, srod nas nam langs) spread through the whole body.

Then (pp. 203–204) how to anoint the la's place by the monthly and daily course and its hour, two ointments, the practice with the day's mantra syllable, and on the 15th an anointing of the crown and the soles; no source is named for the two tables.

**Moxa, the dates to avoid (pp. 255–257, img. 277–279).** Moxa (མེ་བཙའ, me btsa'): among the cautions (p. 255), the la's date is to be known, and the la's place and the slayer weekday (གཤེད་གཟའ, gshed gza') avoided; «ཚེས་གཅིག་དྲུག་དང་བཅོ་ལྔ་དང་། བཅོ་བརྒྱད་ཉེར་གཉིས་སུམ་ཅུ་ཉིན།», on the 1st, 6th, 15th, 18th, 22nd and 30th, moxa, bloodletting (གཏར་བ, gtar ba) and surgery are avoided; and on the fourteen burning dates (བསྲེག་ཚེས, bsreg tshes), made by a date meeting a weekday, bloodletting and moxa are avoided even for a good purpose. The table of p. 256 (img. 278, read on the scan; Yigdzin-1 drops a sheep-to-bird «སངས» and reads «བླ» for the dog's «ཟླ»):

| Birth year | rat | ox | tiger | hare | dragon | snake | horse | sheep | monkey | bird | dog | pig |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| la weekday (བླ་གཟའ, bla gza') | Wed (ལྷག, lhag) | Sat (སྤེན, spen) | Thu (ཕུར, phur) | Thu | Sun (ཉི, nyi) | Tue (དམར, dmar) | Tue | Fri (སངས, sangs) | Fri | Fri | Mon (ཟླ, zla) | Wed |
| life-force weekday (སྲོག་གཟའ, srog gza') | Tue | Wed | Sat | Sat | Wed | Fri | Fri | Mon | Thu | Thu | Wed | Tue |
| slayer weekday (གཤེད་གཟའ, gshed gza') | Sat | Thu | Fri | Fri | Thu | Wed | Wed | Thu | Tue | Tue | Thu | Sat |

Then «བོད་ཀྱི་རྩིས་དང་མཐུན་པར་ཚེས། ༡། ༦། ༡༥། ༡༨། ༢༢། ༣༠་བཅས་ལ་མེ་བཙའ་འདེབ་མི་རུང་བ་ཡིན།», "in agreement with the Tibetan reckoning, moxa is not to be set on the 1st, 6th, 15th, 18th, 22nd and 30th" (the same six dates as p. 255 spells out in words; Yigdzin-1 reads ༡༢ and ༢༠ for ༢༢ and ༣༠), and the fourteen burning dates, two for each weekday, fifteen days apart:

| Weekday | Sunday | Monday | Tuesday | Wednesday | Thursday | Friday | Saturday |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Burning dates | 12, 27 | 11, 26 | 10, 25 | 3, 18 | 6, 21 | 2, 17 | 7, 22 |

In this font ༢ and ༣ differ only by a small hook, and the scan is 1600 px wide: Sunday's 12, Wednesday's 3, Friday's 2 and Saturday's 22 are read against the printed «༢༢» and «༣༠» of the line above and against the fifteen-day spacing of the other pairs; BDRC's etext has Friday 3 (below, *Open*). For the gold-needle moxa (གསེར་མེ, gser me, p. 257): for planet illness (གཟའ་ནད, gza' nad) and fainting with pain arising on the Tibetan dates 4, 8, 11, 15, 18, 22, 25 and 29, the gold needle is set on a good date, twice in two successive years. No source is named for these tables beyond "the Tibetan reckoning".

The rest of KL holds nothing more on dates (etext): «ཚེས» (date) occurs elsewhere only in the dates of the front matter and in passing; «ན་ཚོད» (age) occurs in ch. 3, the massage suited to each age, not a reckoning by age.

### Open

Open questions 58–64 ([open-questions.md](open-questions.md)), in order: the
source of KL's la courses; of its weekdays by birth animal; «ལྒང་བ»;
Friday's burning date; the English books; RS and SY; the Russian *Йога
звука*.

### Searched and not found

- The Library of Congress catalogue (catalog.loc.gov, loc.gov/books, id.loc.gov), 2026-10-12: a Cloudflare check and a 503; its records were read only as copied into Open Library and VIAF.
- WorldCat (search.worldcat.org), 2026-10-12: a fielded search asks to sign in, a plain one to accept the terms first; not used.
- 84000 (84000.co search, "Chenagtsang"), 2026-10-12: no result.
- His name in Tibetan script in a library's authority record: none; VIAF's Tibetan heading is Wikidata's.
- *The Science of Mantra Healing by Interdependence* on BDRC: a title search for *rten 'brel sngags* finds only older texts.
- The etexts of GC (IE0OPI430023E3), the poems (IE0OPI85F3D3D1) and RS (IE0OPI126D5130): `etextchunks` answers 403 (search only); RS's scans are lent on archive.org, not opened here.
- A list of his books on his own site (drnida.com/books): the page holds no list.


## What his works cite (RD8)

Every classical passage that Dr. Nida Chenagtsang's works quote or name for the la (བླ, bla), the life-spirit, for the life force (སྲོག, srog), and for health and illness by date, month, season, hour or age; read 2026-10-12. Sigla as in RD7's section above: **KL** his *Manual of Massage* (2001; BDRC MW30519, scans I1KG21795, printed page = image − 22), **GC** his *Selected Essays on Medicine* (1999; MW21663, restricted), **RS** the Rebkong (རེབ་གོང, reb gong) star-text anthology he co-edited (MW1KG6014, scans lent). His own text is quoted no further than the facts need; the classical lines he quotes are given whole.

How it was read. KL: the whole of BDRC's etext (IE0OPIACDDA818, computer input) searched for every quotation marker («ཞེས», «ལས།», «རྒྱུད», «ལྕགས་པར་མ», «གསུངས», «ཤོག་ངོས», the footnotes) and for «བླ», «སྲོག», «ཚེས», «ན་ཚོད», «གཟའ», «དུས»; the 13 pages with footnotes and every quotation listed; the pages that bear on the la or on time read on the scan by eye, enlarged 2–5× (img. 25, 46, 77, 83, 84), with Yigdzin-1 (through the GPU slot) and the etext as witnesses; img. 221–226 and 277–279 as RD7 read them. GC and RS: BDRC's "search this collection" box in the etext viewer (their etexts answer 403 to download), so **snippets only**, about 120 characters each, in BDRC's Wylie (the script here is converted from it); the page numbers there are BDRC's, not the printed ones. The classical works KL names were then found on BDRC and the quoted lines located in their etexts (machine text, not read on scans: that is RD9's).

| 4 | The Four Tantras, the Final Tantra (ཕྱི་རྒྱུད, phyi rgyud), on the pulse of the la as a sign of life | GC BDRC pp. 185 and 196 (snippets); not in KL | the la pulse stays, shifts or flees, and life with it | not located in this reading |

Not about the la or time, listed so that the inventory is whole: KL also cites the Root Tantra ch. 6, *the examples* («རླུང་ལ་དྲོ་སར་ཡིད་འོང་གྲོགས་བསྟེན་ཞིང་།», p. 58, note 1, "Iron-block" p. 12; MW3CN5036 etext p. 26), the Explanatory Tantra ch. 13, *daily conduct* (the benefit of massage and the bath, p. 98, "Iron-block" p. 78; MW3CN5036 etext pp. 60–62) and its chapter on the treatment proper (p. 59, "Iron-block" p. 95), a Dunhuang (ཏུན་ཧོང, tun hong) medical fragment (p. 21), Longchen Rabjampa's (ཀློང་ཆེན་རབ་འབྱམས་པ, klong chen rab 'byams pa) two treasuries (p. 4), Lhundingpa Dudtsi Gyurme's (ལྷུན་ལྡིངས་བདུད་རྩི་འགྱུར་མེད, lhun ldings bdud rtsi 'gyur med) manual on stone massage (p. 3), the vajra-stick treatises (pp. 102, 129, 230–235) and Mipham's medical collection on gems (p. 194).

### The passages in KL

**The source of the la's anointing (KL p. 3, img. 25, read on the scan).** The introduction says the anointing of the channel wheels and of the la's places is not found stated in the medical texts but gathered from tantric ones, and its two footnotes name the works:

> རྩ་འཁོར་① དང་བླ་གནས་② ལ་བྱུག་པ་བྱ་ཐབས་ཀྱི་སྐོར་ནི་གསོ་རིག་གཞུང་དུ་དངོས་སུ་བཤད་པ་མ་མཐོང་ཡང་སྔགས་རྒྱུད་ཀྱི་གཞུང་ཁ་ཅིག་ལས་ནང་དོན་གྱི་གནད་ཚང་བར་ཕྱོགས་བསྡུ་བྱས་པ་ཡིན།།
>
> ② བླ་གནས་ཀྱི་བྱུག་པ་བྱ་ཐབས་ནི་འཇུ་མི་ཕམ་རྣམ་རྒྱལ་རྒྱ་མཚོས་བྲིས་པའི་འདོད་པའི་བསྟན་བཅོས་འཇིག་རྟེན་ཀུན་ཏུ་དགའི་བའི་གཏེར་ཞེས་པར་བཤད་པ་གཞི་འཛིན་བྱས་པ་ཡིན།

"The way to anoint the la's places rests on what Ju Mipham Namgyal Gyatso's *Treatise on Passion, a Treasure that Pleases the Whole World* says." The print has «དགའི་བའི» (read at 4× on the scan; Yigdzin-1 and the etext agree), for «དགའ་བའི». BDRC's etext labels the two footnotes «@» and «༤»; the scan has ① and ②. Note ① names Rigdzin Gödem's treasure text for the channel wheels.

**Mipham and the la's moving (KL p. 24, img. 46, read on the scan)**, in the history of massage: «འཇུ་མི་ཕམ་རྣམ་རྒྱལ་རྒྱ་མཚོས་ཀྱང་མཛེས་བཟོའི་སྐོར་ཕྱོགས་བསྡུ་རགས་ཙམ་དང་། ཁྱད་པར་བླ་འཕོ་བ་དང་བསྟུན་ནས་བླ་གནས་བསྐུ་མཉེ་བྱེད་སྟངས་སོགས་བརྩམས་ནས་སྤེལ་གནང་…»: Mipham wrote a short collection on beauty care and, above all, how to massage the la's places following the la's moving. So KL ties the la's course to Mipham's treatise, though ch. 10 itself (pp. 198–204) names no source for its two tables (RD7).

**The humours by season and hour, uncited (KL p. 55, img. 77, read on the scan).** For the healthy: wind (རླུང, rlung) gathers in *so ga* (སོ་ག, so ga), the season between spring and the rainy summer, rises in summer, is pacified in autumn, and in the day rises in the evening and at dawn; bile (མཁྲིས་པ, mkhris pa) gathers in summer, rises in autumn, is pacified in early winter, rises at midday and midnight («ཉིན་གུང་མཚན་གུང»); phlegm (བད་ཀན, bad kan) gathers in late winter, rises in spring, is pacified in *so ga*, rises at dusk. Massage at the gathering time prevents, at the rising time cures. No text is named; it restates the Four Tantras (item 1).

**The seasons, with the Four Tantras (KL p. 61, img. 83, and p. 62, img. 84, read on the scan).** The time for massage depends on the oil: «ཏིལ་མར་དབྱར་དང་། ཞུན་མར་དབྱར་དང་དཔྱིད་ལ་བཟང་བ» (sesame oil in summer, clarified butter in summer and spring, as printed: the first «དབྱར» is clear on the scan, though p. 46 gives clarified butter for winter); then three quotations from the Explanatory Tantra and one from the Root Tantra:

> གསོ་རིག་བཤད་རྒྱུད་དུ།① དེ་ཚེ་མཚན་མོ་རིང་བས་བཀྲེས་འགྱུར་ཏེ།། དེ་ཡིས་ལུས་ཟུངས་བྲི་བར་འགྱུར་བས་ཕྱིར།། ཏིལ་མར་གྱིས་བྱུག་ཤ་ཁུ་སྣུམ་པ་ཟ།། པགས་པའི་གོས་དང་ལྷམ་ནི་རྟག་ཏུ་བགོ། དུགས་དང་ཉི་མའི་འོད་ལ་རན་པར་བསྲོ།། ཞེས་གསུངས་པ་ལྟར་དུ། …
>
> སོ་ག་ཉི་མའི་འོད་ཟེར་རབ་ཚ་བས།། སྟོབས་འཕྲོག་ཕྱིར་ན་མངར་ཡང་སྣུམ་བསིལ་བཟའ།། …
>
> དབྱར་དུས་མཁའ་ལ་སྤྲིན་འདུས་ཆུ་ཡིས་བརླན། རླུང་དང་གྲང་བ་ས་རླངས་ཆུ་རྙོག་ཅན།། མེ་དྲོད་གནོད་པས་མེ་དྲོད་བསྐྱེད་པ་བསྟེན།། དང་བོ་རོ་གསུམ་ཡང་དྲོ་སྣུམ་བཅས་བཟའ། ཞེས་དང་། གསོ་རིག་རྩ་རྒྱུད་དུ།② རླུང་ནད་དབྱར་དུས་དགོང་དང་ཐོ་རེངས་ལྡང་།།
>
> ① གསོ་རིག་རྒྱུད་བཞི་ལྕགས་པར་མའི་བཤད་རྒྱུད་དུས་སུ་སྤྱོད་པའི་ལེའུ་ཤོག་ངོས་༥༠སྟེང་གསལ། ② གསོ་རིག་རྒྱུད་བཞི་ལྕགས་པར་མའི་རྩ་རྒྱུད་གནས་ལུགས་ནད་གཞི་ལེའུ་ཤོག་ངོས༡༠སྟེང་གསལ།

"Then the nights are long, so one grows hungry; that wears down the body's constituents; anoint with sesame oil, eat meat broth and fat; always wear skin clothes and boots; warm yourself in moderation with heat (དུགས, dugs) and in the sun" (early winter); "in *so ga* the sun's rays are very hot and steal strength, so eat sweet, light, oily and cool food" (KL: hence oiling then too); "in summer clouds gather in the sky and it is damp with rain; wind, cold, the earth's vapour and muddy water harm the digestive heat, so rely on what raises it; eat the first three tastes, light, warm and with oil"; and the Root Tantra, "wind illness rises in summer, in the evening and at dawn". Notes: Explanatory Tantra, *conduct by the season*, p. 50; Root Tantra, *the basis of illness as it is*, p. 10 (both read on the scan; Yigdzin-1 reads ༡༠༠ for the second, the scan has ༡༠). On p. 62: «ཉིན་ཞག་གཅིག་ལས། དགོང་དང་ཐོ་རེངས་ལ་བཟང་སྟེ། རླུང་ནད་དགོང་དང་ཐོ་རེངས་ལྡང་ཞེས་གསུངས་སོ།།», within the day, evening and dawn are good for massage, since "wind illness rises in the evening and at dawn".

**The ages, uncited (KL p. 40, etext).** Child to 16 (the newborn of phlegm's nature, from about three of wind's), youth 16–25 and adult to 70 (bile's), old after 70 (wind's). No text named; the Root Tantra ch. 3 has the line «རྒས་པ་རླུང་མི་དར་མ་མཁྲིས་པའི་མི། བྱིས་པ་བད་ཀན་མི» (MW3CN5036, etext p. 22, machine text): the old are wind people, adults bile, children phlegm.

**The la and the life (KL p. 200, img. 222; p. 63, img. 85; p. 239, img. 261; from the etext).** Tibetan medicine counts «བླ་ཚེ་སྲོག», the la, the lifespan (ཚེ, tshe) and the life force, as a vital element; a wound, pressure or blow on the la's place can endanger life, so binding, bloodletting, moxa and hard pressure are avoided there (p. 200). Before massage the ring fingers and ring toes are tied with red thread so that the la stays in place and no spirit illness enters (p. 63, «བླ་རྩ་བསྡམ་པ»). Stick therapy is not done on the la's places, for striking them risks the la's moving (p. 239). None of these names a text.

**Moxa (KL pp. 255–257, RD7).** The six dates «ཚེས་གཅིག་དྲུག་དང་བཅོ་ལྔ་དང་། བཅོ་བརྒྱད་ཉེར་གཉིས་སུམ་ཅུ་ཉིན།» are cast as a verse line but named only as "in agreement with the Tibetan reckoning"; BDRC's full-text search finds the line in no other work (below).

### What the classical texts say beside KL's lines (machine text)

From MW3CN5036's etext and BDRC's search snippets only, for RD9 to read on the scans. The Root Tantra ch. 3 goes on from the humours' hours to «འཚོ་བ་གསུམ་ཟད་འདུ་བ་གཤེད་དུ་བབས། … དུས་འདས་རླུང་ནད་སྲོག་རྟེན་ཆད་པ…», the signs of death, among them the life force's support cut (p. 23). KL's Root Tantra line on p. 199 reads «མཁྲིས་པ་སྟོན་དུས་ཉིན་དགོང་མཚན་དགོང་ལྡང», where MW3CN5036 and the collected works of Pema Karpo (པདྨ་དཀར་པོ, padma dkar po; MW10736, BDRC search) read «ཉིན་གུང་མཚན་གུང», as KL's own p. 55 does. The Explanatory Tantra ch. 14 opens with the divisions of time (the six seasons, the sun's northern and southern courses, the tastes each season strengthens) before the conduct KL quotes; its early-winter lines, as the commentary of Byangpa Namgyal Drakpa Zangpo (བྱང་པ་རྣམ་རྒྱལ་གྲགས་པ་བཟང་པོ, byang pa rnam rgyal grags pa bzang po; MW27496, BDRC search) gives them, run «…ཟས་ཉུང་གྱུར་ན་ལུས་ཟུངས་འགྲིབ་པར་འགྱུར། དེ་ཕྱིར་དང་པོའི་རོ་གསུམ་བསྟེན་པར་བྱ། དེ་ཚེ་མཚན་མོ་རིང་བས་བཀྲེས་འགྱུར་ཏེ། ཏིལ་མར་གྱིས་བྱུག་ཤ་ཁུ་སྣུམ་པ་བཟའ། ལྤགས་པའི་གོས་དང་ལྷམ་ནི་རྟག་ཏུ་བགོ།…»: KL's second line «དེ་ཡིས་ལུས་ཟུངས་བྲི་བར་འགྱུར་བས་ཕྱིར» is not in that place.

### KL's tables against the older works

**The weekdays by birth animal (KL p. 256) are WB's.** All 36 of KL's la, life-force and slayer weekdays (བླ་གཟའ, སྲོག་གཟའ, གཤེད་གཟའ; bla gza', srog gza', gshed gza') agree with WB vol. 2, p. 330, as [personal-mansions.md](personal-mansions.md) gives them read on the scan (*Result*). KL still names no source.

**The monthly course (KL pp. 201–202) is WB's Phugpa list with four places changed.** Against WB vol. 2, pp. 303–304 ([lunar-dates.md](lunar-dates.md), the person's column): 26 dates agree (RD10 below, reading WB on the scan, counts 25, the 10th and 30th as near; the places are the same in both counts); KL has the knee on the 4th (WB the waist), the shoulder blade on the 10th (WB the top of the shoulder), the crown and whole body on the 15th (WB the shoulder blade), the instep on the 29th (WB the shin), and adds the sole on the 30th. KL's syllables (ཨ ཨ ཨི ཨི ཨུ ཨུ ཨེ ཨེ རི རི ལི ལི ཨོ ཨོ ཨཾ) are not WB's Kālacakra list.

**The same course in older medical books, found by BDRC's full-text search** (snippets; not cited by KL). KL's place words «ལྟག་པ་ཟ་འགྲམ» and «རྣ་ལྟག་ཟ་འགྲམ» are found under the heading «བླ་གནས་འཕོ་ཚུལ། གཙུག་ལག་རིག་བྱེད་ལུགས་ཀྱི», "the la's moving, by the system of the Tsuglag Rigje (གཙུག་ལག་རིག་བྱེད, gtsug lag rig byed)", in Deumar's *Precious Garland* (item 6); in Situ Wön Karma Ngedön's (སི་ཏུ་དབོན་ཀརྨ་ངེས་དོན, si tu dbon karma nges don) *Garland of Deathless Jewels* (འཆི་མེད་ནོར་བུའི་ཕྲེང་བ, 'chi med nor bu'i phreng ba; MW24827), with people and horses side by side («…༡༣ མི་ཡི་སྐེ་མགུལ་སྟེང་ན་གནས། … ༡༤ མི་ཡི་རྣ་ལྟག་ཟ་འགྲམ་ལ། … ༡༥ མི་ཡི་ལུས་ཀུན་ཁྱབ་པར་གནས།»); in a Drigung (འབྲི་གུང, 'bri gung) text on striking the la-dagger (བླ་ཕུར, bla phur) at the la's place (MW3KG218_O3KG218_HNUNIO: «…བཅུ་གསུམ་ལ་སྐེ་མགུལ། བཅུ་བཞི་རྣ་ལྟག་དང་ཟ་འགྲམ། བཅོ་ལྔ་ལུས་ཀུན་ལ་ཁྱབ། བཅུ་དྲུག་ལ་རྣ་ལྟག་ཟ་འགྲམ། བཅུ་བདུན་སྐེ་མཇིང་། བཅོ་བརྒྱད་ལ་ལག་མཐིལ། བཅུ་དགུ་ལ་ལག་ངར། ཉི་ཤུ་ལ་དཔུང་སོག་སྟེང་…»); and in modern textbooks (1997, 2013, 2016, 2019).

In Deumar's book (etext pp. 605–608, printed pp. 589–592; good OCR, not seen on a scan) the bloodletting instruction's third head is the time: the la's places of the four times, year, month, day and hour, the month's place by the birth year, the day's place in the systems of Cakrasaṃvara (བདེ་མཆོག, bde mchog), the Kālacakra (དུས་འཁོར, dus 'khor) and many others; then one course in verse (the 1st the big toe, the instep and the left of the crown …), and a table that sets side by side, date by date, the Kālacakra system, Zurkhar's (ཟུར་མཁར, zur mkhar) system, the Tsuglag Rigje system, the system "of the southern treasure, the treasure of existence" (ལྷོ་གཏེར་སྲིད་པའི་གཏེར, lho gter srid pa'i gter) with hours, a "house" system, the new Chinese reckoning (རྒྱ་རྩིས་གསར་མ, rgya rtsis gsar ma) and livestock. The OCR scrambles the table's columns, but the Tsuglag Rigje column visibly runs «རྐང་པའི་མཐེ་བོང» (1st), «རྐང་པའི་བོལ» (2nd) … «སྐེ་མགུལ། རྣ་ལྟག་ཟ་འགྲམ། ལུས་སྤྱིར་ཁྱབ།» (13th–15th) … «ལྟག་པ་ཟ་འགྲམ»: KL's course. Its verse on the times to avoid (etext p. 605, machine text):

> གསུམ་པ་གཏར་བའི་དུས་གནད་བརྟག་པ་ལ། །སྤང་བླང་གཉིས་ཡིན་དང་པོ་སྤང་བྱ་ལ། །ལོ་ཟླ་ཞག་དུས་དུས་བཞིའི་བླ་གནས་བཙི། །ལོ་ནི་སྤར་ཁ་གང་ལ་གནས་པ་ཡི། །དཔྱད་སྤང་དཔེར་ན་ལི་ལ་མེ་དཔྱད་དང་། །དེ་བཞིན་མགོ་ལག་རྩིབ་རྐང་གསང་བ་སྒྱུར། །ཟླ་བའི་བླ་གནས་བྱི་ཕག་ལོ་པ་ནི། །དགུན་ཟླ་འབྲིང་པོར་མི་གཏར་གླང་ལུག་བྱ། །དབྱར་ར་ལ་འཛེམ་སྟག་འབྲུག་དགུན་མཐར་སྤོངས། །ཡོས་སྦྲུལ་དབྱར་ཐ་རྟ་ལ་དཔྱིད་ར་མིན། །སྤྲེལ་ལོ་དབྱར་འབྲིང་ཁྱི་ལོ་སྟོན་མཐར་སྤོངས། །ཞག་གི་བླ་གནས་བདེ་མཆོག་ཁ་སྦྱོར་དང་། །དུས་འཁོར་ལ་སོགས་བཀའ་གཏེར་མན་ངག་ལུགས། །འདྲ་མིན་མང་རྣམས་ཀུན་རྫོབ་བདེན་མཉམ་ཕྱིར། །འདོར་ལེན་མི་རིགས་ཀུན་ཀྱང་ཁྱོན་གཅིག་བཤད། །

"Third, the time of bloodletting: what to avoid and what to take. To avoid: reckon the la's places of the four times, year, month, day and hour. The year: by the trigram (སྤར་ཁ, spar kha) in which it stands the treatment is avoided, fire treatment on the trigram li (ལི, li), for example; and likewise the head, hands, ribs, feet and secret parts (the sense of «སྒྱུར» here is not clear). The month's la place: those born in rat and pig years are not bled in the middle winter month; ox, sheep and bird avoid the first summer month; tiger and dragon give up the last winter month; hare and snake the last summer month; horse not the first spring month; monkey gives up the middle summer month, dog the last autumn month. For the day's la place the systems of Cakrasaṃvara's union, the Kālacakra and others, of the transmitted, treasure and instruction lines, differ much; since all are equal in conventional truth, none is to be rejected or taken alone, and all are set out together."

**The hours (KL pp. 202–203).** WB vol. 2, p. 304 (img. 312; the local Yigdzin-1 reading, machine text) gives the hours within any day as «ནམ་ལངས་རྐང་གཉིས་ཉི་ཤར་མཇིང་། །དྲོས་ཆུང་ལྟག་ཁུང་དྲོས་ཆེན་མཆུ། །ཉི་ཕྱེད་བྲང་གཞུང་ཕྱེད་ཡོལ་ལྟེ། །མྱུར་ཁ་སྙིང་ག་མྱུར་ཆུང་རྒྱབ། །ཉི་ནུབ་རྐང་པ་གཉིས་ལ་གནས། །ས་སྲོས་ནས་ནི་ནམ་ལངས་བར། །ལྟེ་བ་ཡན་ཆད་ཁྱབ་པར་གནས།». KL's course follows the same order (the neck at sunrise, lips and nape in the morning, the chest at midday, the belly after midday, the heart in the afternoon, the back at dusk, the whole body through the night), and where WB has both feet at daybreak («རྐང་གཉིས»), KL has «ལྒང་བ».

### What GC says (BDRC snippets only)

GC is restricted on BDRC and its etext answers 403; its viewer's search gave these snippets (BDRC's page numbers; by the contents' start pages the printed page is about the BDRC page − 20, not seen on a scan):

- **The la channel and the life (BDRC pp. 185, 196, in the essay on medicine and Dharma joined).** «…བླ་ཡི་བཤད་པ་འདི་ཕྱིས་སུ་གསོ་རིག་བཤད་རྒྱུད་དུ་རྩ་གསུམ་ལས་གཅིག་ལ། གཅིག་ནི་བླ་དང་འདྲ་སྟེ་འཆམ་པ་ཡིན། ཞེས་དང་། ཕྱི་རྒྱུད་དུ། ཚེའི་རྟགས་བླ་ཡི་རྩ་ལ་བརྟག་པ་ནི། སེམས་ལ་རང་དབང་མ་ཐོབ་ཕྱིར…» and «…སྲིད་པའི་ལུས་ངན་བླངས་པ་ཡིས། ཚེ་སྲོག་རྟེན་དུ་བླ་རྩ་འཁྱམ་པོ་བྱུང་། བླ་རྩ་གནས་ན་བླ་ཚེ་མལ་དུ་གནས། བླ་རྩ་འགྱུར་ལྡོག་བྱེད་ན་ཚེ་མི་བརྟན། བླ་རྩ་བྲོས་ན་བླ་ཚེ་ཡར་ཏེ་འཆི། ཚེ་ཆོག་འཆི་བསླུས་ཁུགས་པ་སྲིད་པ་ཙམ།»: the Explanatory Tantra's line (here «འཆམ་པ», KL «འཁྱམ་པ») and the Final Tantra's on the pulse of the la as the sign of life: having taken the body of existence, the wandering la channel is the support of the lifespan and life force; while it stays, the la's life stays in place; if it shifts, life is unstable; if it flees, the la's life departs and one dies, and a long-life rite or a ransom from death may at most bring it back. Then «…གཏར་བསྲེག་སོགས་ཀྱི་དུས་སུ་བླ་གནས་བརྩི་བ་སོགས་སྨན་གཞུང་གི་ནང་དོན་གལ་ཆེན་ཞིག་ཏུ་མངོན…»: reckoning the la's places at the time of bloodletting and cautery shows as an important matter of the medical texts.
- **The la's course in the massage essay (BDRC pp. 224–225).** «…བདེ་བ་རྒྱས་པར་མཆོག་ཏུ་གྱུར་པའི་གདམས་ངག་ལ་དཀར་ཕྱོགས་ཚེས་གཅིག་ནས་བརྩམས་ཏེ་སྐྱེས་བུའི་བྱང་སེམས་རྐྱང་མའི་དབང་ནས་འབྱུང་ཕྱིར་གཡོན་དང་བུད་མེད་ཀྱི་བྱང་སེམས་རོ་མའི་དབང་ལས་འབྱུང་ཕྱིར་གཡས་ནས་སྟེ། ༡ རྐང…» … «…༡༣ སྐེ་མགུལ། ཨོ ༡༤ རྣ་ལྟག་ཟ་འགྲམ། ཨོ། ༡༥ ཚེས་བཅོ་ལྔར་སྤྱི་བོ་ལུས་ཀུན་ཁྱབ་པ་དང་། མར་ངོས་གོང་ལས་ལྡོག་སྟེ། ཚེས་བཅུ་དྲུག་ནས་རྣ་ལྟག་ནས་རྐང་བོལ་བར་དུ་རིམ་གྱིས…»: KL's course and syllables, given as an instruction for strength, radiance, long life and the growth of bliss, starting on the 1st of the waxing half, on a man's left because his vital drop (བྱང་སེམས, byang sems) moves in the left channel (རྐྱང་མ, rkyang ma), on a woman's right because hers moves in the right (རོ་མ, ro ma); in the waning half the course returns from behind the ear to the instep. Beside it the la channel is named again as the Explanatory Tantra's channel "like the la", and «…ལོ་རྟགས་དང་གཟའི་འཕྲོད་སྦྱོར་དང་བསྟུན་ནས་སྲོག་གཟའ་དང་བླ་གཟར་འཕྲོད་ན་འང་ཤིན་ཏུ་ཕན་པ་ལྟར་གཤེད་གཟའ་ལ་སྤང་བར་བྱའོ།»: as massage on the life-force and la weekdays of one's birth animal is very beneficial, the slayer weekday is avoided; KL's table of p. 256 follows (BDRC p. 225). No source shows in the snippets.
- **Seasons and dates elsewhere in GC.** The Root Tantra's wind line (BDRC p. 229); the Explanatory Tantra's «སོས་ཀ་རྩུབ་ཅིང་མཚན་མོ་ཐུང་བས་ན…» on sleep by day in summer (p. 98); in the dream essay a practice done from the 1st to the 8th of the month (p. 103); in the snow-frog essay the frogs' mating from the 3rd to the 23rd of the three summer months (p. 106), about the animal, not health. The stick-therapy essay forbids striking the la's places (p. 241), as KL does.

### What RS holds (BDRC snippets only)

RS's viewer search («བླ་གནས», «བླ», «ནད», «སྨན», «གཏར») shows (BDRC pages): a reckoning of the la's places by date, «བླ་གནས་ཀྱི་རྩིས། ཚེས་གཅིག་རྐང་པའི་མཐེ་བོང་ལ་གནས། གཉིས་རྐང་པའི་ཚིགས། གསུམ་བླ་ཁུག བཞི་རྐེད་པ། ལྔ་ལྕེ། དྲུག་རོ་སྟོད། བདུན་ལག་ཚིགས…» (pp. 104, 169; on p. 169 just after a passage ending «…བཻ་དཀར་ལས་གསུངས།», "so says the *White Beryl*"), which is not KL's course, with «…གནམ་སྟོང་ལ་ལུས་ཁྱབ་སར་གནས། དེ་དག་ཏུ་གཏར་སྲེག་ངན།» and «གནས་དེ་རྣམས་སུ་གཏར་ཁ་དང་མེ་བཙའི་ལས་རྣམས་སྤང་ངོ»: no bloodletting or moxa at those places (pp. 104, 169); the livestock's la places (p. 110); a man's la starting from the sole and going right, a woman's left, and la places by the hour (p. 143); la places by the day's animal, «བླ་གནས་རྩི་ཚུལ། ༡ བྱི་བའི་ཉི་མར་མིག་ལ་གནས། ༢ གླང་གི་ཉི…» (p. 244); the four perilous dates of every month, «གནམ་གང་། ཚེས་བརྒྱད། བཅོ་ལྔ། ཉེར་གཉིས», on which falling ill is bad (p. 220); days unfit for treating the sick (pp. 102, 166, 241); the weekdays as the la weekdays of the castes (pp. 87–88, 146–147, 223–225). None of these is cited by Dr. Chenagtsang in the works read; they show that the anthology carries la courses of its own.

### The facts, for RD9

- His works rest on three layers. Named classical sources: the Four Tantras for the humours by season and hour (Root Tantra ch. 3), conduct by season (Explanatory Tantra ch. 14) and the la channel (Explanatory Tantra ch. 4); GC adds the Final Tantra's la pulse. Named for the la's anointing: Mipham's *Treatise on Passion*. Unnamed: the monthly and hourly la courses, the weekdays by animal, the ages and the moxa dates.
- Of the unnamed ones, the weekdays are WB's (36 of 36); the monthly course matches WB's Phugpa list in 26 dates and the Tsuglag Rigje system in Deumar's *Precious Garland*, a book KL itself uses; the hours follow WB's list, with «ལྒང་བ» for WB's both feet at daybreak; the six moxa dates are found nowhere else on BDRC.
- RD9's pages: MW3CN5036, etext pp. 22–23, 26, 38–39, 60–63 (and a second print of the Four Tantras to check them); Mipham's treatise in I1KG8551 from img. 536; Deumar's printed pp. 587–592 (lent scans); the Final Tantra's la-pulse passage, not yet located.

Deumar's Tsuglag Rigje column; Mipham's treatise; «ལྕགས་པར་མ»; «ཉིན་དགོང་མཚན་དགོང»;
KL p. 61's second line; GC's source for its la course. KL's «ལྒང་བ» is

its six moxa dates question 79; question 58 is answered further (below,
*The facts, for RD9*).

## WB on health (RD10)

What WB says of the la (བླ, bla, the life-spirit), the life force (སྲོག, srog) and illness outside its illness reckoning (ch. 25, RD12 above): in the framework of the element reckoning (vol. 1, ch. 20, pp. 218–251, img. 228–261) and its primer (ch. 21, pp. 252–258, img. 262–268); in the readings of ch. 33 by lunar date, weekday and day animal (vol. 2, pp. 297–304, 308–312, 356–359); in the year of age's mewa (vol. 1, pp. 403–405, img. 413–415); and on the enemy and grave mansions (vol. 2, pp. 330, 338, img. 338, 346). Read 2026-10-12. Sigla: WB, and KL for Dr. Chenagtsang's *Manual of Massage* (RD7's section above).

**A correction to the row.** The enemy and grave mansions are on pp. 330 and 338 of **vol. 2** (img. 338, 346), not vol. 1, as [personal-mansions.md](personal-mansions.md) has them; vol. 1's pp. 330 and 338 are in ch. 22 (RD13).

**In short.**

- **KL's tables are WB's** but for the six moxa dates and the syllables. The la, life-force and slayer weekdays by birth animal (KL p. 256) are WB vol. 2, p. 330, all thirty-six cells. The la's course by date (KL pp. 201–202) is WB's Phugpa list (vol. 2, p. 303) in 25 of 30 places; KL's knee, crown and instep on the 4th, 15th and 29th keep the order where WB prints the waist, the shoulder blade and the shin. The course by hour (KL pp. 202–203) is WB's course within the day (p. 304), in the same order; WB has both feet at daybreak, where KL prints «ལྒང་བ». Friday's burning dates are the 2nd and 17th in WB, as on KL's scan (open questions 58–61).
- **Illness by lunar date** is eight readings in a round of eight (dates 1–8, the 9th as the 1st, … the 30th as the 6th; vol. 2, pp. 297–302), the same readings as ch. 25's eight goddesses of the date (vol. 1, pp. 449–450); quoted in full below. Falling ill on the 8th, 15th, 22nd and 30th is dangerous.
- **Bloodletting and moxa** by date, weekday, day animal, burning date, the la's place and the year of age's mewa are gathered in one table below; ch. 34 (vol. 2, pp. 403–404) restates the dates (4, 8, 29, 30 bad; 11, 18 good) and weekdays.
- **Illness by weekday** (vol. 2, pp. 308–312): each weekday's lines on falling ill, quoted below.
- **The la** in WB's primer is the mother of the life force (vol. 1, p. 253), the element that gives birth to the life force's element; the life force is the year animal's (tiger and hare wood, …); the four aspects of month, day and hour are found as the year's (p. 255). Ch. 20 (pp. 218–251) has no table of the la or the life force; it gives the body as the five elements (flesh earth, blood water, breath wind, bone iron, sinews wood; the five organs, p. 232), each element's spirits and illnesses (p. 236), the mansions as senders of illness (p. 234), and the twelve stages of a life with the bird for illness (pp. 234–235).
- **The enemy and grave mansions** (vol. 2, pp. 330, 338) have no reading in WB; "every work avoided" is said of the enemy weekday only, and nothing of illness is said of either mansion.
- **The year of age's mewa** (vol. 1, pp. 403–405): the seven-red, by its colour alone, avoids bloodletting and moxa (p. 403) and brings them (p. 404); by the natal mewa (p. 405), the two-black on the seven-red makes moxa good and bloodletting bad; pp. 401–404 are not in [year-of-life.md](year-of-life.md).

### How it was read (RD10)

- **The etext** (IE0OPI51524892, page = image) for the outline of ch. 20 and 21, for finding every «བླ», «སྲོག» and «ནད» in them, and as a witness.
- **Yigdzin-1** over every page of ch. 20–21 not read before (vol. 1 img. 228–268 but 259, 263, already read for RD11) and img. 413–415, into `wb/vol1/yigdzin/`; over vol. 2 img. 313–320, 346 and 364–367 into `wb/yigdzin/`. The earlier readings of vol. 2 img. 305–312 (Yigdzin-1 and MITRA, 2026-10-03) were reused.
- **MITRA** as witness over the quoted pages that had none, vol. 1 img. 242, 244–247, 252, 262, 263, 265 and 413–415 (into `wb/vol1/mitra/`) and vol. 2 img. 316–320 (into `wb/mitra/`); `disagree.py` over each pair, and every flag that falls in a quoted line read on the scan (MITRA agrees on 88–97% of the syllables of each page; most of its flags here are its own: ལྟ for ལྷ, བཙན for བཙོན, dropped lines).
- **On the scan**: every passage quoted below was put against the scan line by line, enlarged 1.7–4× (both volumes are scanned about 700 px wide), and the flags of `disagree.py` read there. Doubts are marked […] or named.
- Already quoted and checked on the scan in other files, and only linked here: the lunar dates' activity lists ([lunar-dates.md](lunar-dates.md)), the la's places ([lunar-dates.md](lunar-dates.md), *Where the la resides*), the weekdays' activity lists ([weekdays.md](weekdays.md)), the day animals' verses ([day-animals.md](day-animals.md)), the personal mansions and weekdays of p. 330 and the works on one's own days of p. 338 ([personal-mansions.md](personal-mansions.md)), the burning dates ([burning-dates.md](burning-dates.md)), the year of age's mewa by the natal mewa ([year-of-life.md](year-of-life.md)).

### KL's tables in WB (open questions 58, 59, 60 and 61, in part)

RD7 found in KL (pp. 201–203, 255–257) the la's course by date and by hour, the la, life-force and slayer weekdays by birth animal, six dates and the burning dates to avoid for moxa, and no source named. WB has all of them but the six dates.

**The la, life-force and slayer weekdays by birth animal (KL p. 256) are WB vol. 2, p. 330 (img. 338), cell for cell.** WB's verse names, from the mouse to the pig, each animal's la weekday (བླ་གཟའ, bla gza'), life-force weekday (སྲོག་གཟའ, srog gza') and, with the slayer mansion, its slayer weekday (གཤེད་གཟའ, gshed gza'); the verse is quoted, read on the scan, in [personal-mansions.md](personal-mansions.md), *Text*:

> ལྷག(༤) སྤེན(༠) ཕུར(༥) གཉིས་ཉི(༡) དམར(༣) གཉིས། །སངས(༦) གསུམ་ཟླ(༢) ལྷག(༤) བླ་གཟའ་རིམ། །དེ་བཞིན་བྱི་བ་ནས་རིམ་པར། །དམར(༣) ལྷག(༤) སྤེན(༠) གཉིས་ལྷག(༤) སངས(༦) གཉིས། །ཟླ་བ(༢) ཕུར(༥) གཉིས་ལྷག(༤) མིག་དམར(༣)། །སོ་སོའི་སྲོག་གཟའ།

and, of the twelve pairs of slayer weekday and slayer mansion, «སྤེན་པ(༠) མོན་གྲེ(༢༢) ཕུར་བུ(༥) མགོ(༤)། །པ་སངས(༦) བྲ་ཉེ(༡) པ་སངས(༦) སྣྲོན(༡༧)། །ཕུར(༥) གྲེ(༡༠) ལྷག(༤) ལག(༥) ལྷག(༤) ཤེ་ས(༢༣)། །ཕུར་བུ(༥) དྲུང་ནི་ནམ་གྲུ(༢༦) དང་། །མིག་དམར(༣) ལྷ་མཚམས(༡༦) དམར(༣) མོན་གྲུ(༢༣)། །ཕུར(༥) དབོ(༡༡) སྤེན(༠) ཁྲ(༡༡) རིམ་པ་བཞིན། །གཤེད་གཟའ་གཤེད་སྐར་ཞེས་བྱ་སྟེ།». Set against KL's table (health.md, RD7's section):

| Birth year | mouse | ox | tiger | hare | dragon | snake | horse | sheep | monkey | bird | dog | pig |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| la weekday, WB p. 330 | Wed | Sat | Thu | Thu | Sun | Tue | Tue | Fri | Fri | Fri | Mon | Wed |
| la weekday, KL p. 256 | Wed | Sat | Thu | Thu | Sun | Tue | Tue | Fri | Fri | Fri | Mon | Wed |
| life-force weekday, WB | Tue | Wed | Sat | Sat | Wed | Fri | Fri | Mon | Thu | Thu | Wed | Tue |
| life-force weekday, KL | Tue | Wed | Sat | Sat | Wed | Fri | Fri | Mon | Thu | Thu | Wed | Tue |
| slayer weekday, WB | Sat | Thu | Fri | Fri | Thu | Wed | Wed | Thu | Tue | Tue | Thu | Sat |
| slayer weekday, KL | Sat | Thu | Fri | Fri | Thu | Wed | Wed | Thu | Tue | Tue | Thu | Sat |

All thirty-six agree. KL's names are WB's (བླ་གཟའ, སྲོག་གཟའ, གཤེད་གཟའ); WB's verse goes on to the life-force, power, obstacle and demon mansions, which KL leaves out. What WB says of these days (p. 330: the la weekday at its strong time for activities, the slayer weekday to be avoided; born in the slayer mansion, a short life) is in [personal-mansions.md](personal-mansions.md), *Readings*. KL's use of them, avoiding moxa on the slayer weekday, is not in WB's verse; WB names bloodletting and moxa by weekday only in the weekdays' own lists (below).

**The la's course by date (KL pp. 201–202) is WB's Phugpa list, vol. 2, p. 303 (img. 311),** the list WB calls the view of Phug pa uncle and nephew (ཕུག་པ་ཁུ་དབོན, phug pa khu dbon), people's column; the list is quoted, read on the scan, in [lunar-dates.md](lunar-dates.md), *People and horses*. KL leaves out the livestock column. Place by place:

| Date | WB p. 303 | KL p. 201 | |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1–3, 5–9, 11–14, 16–28 | big toe; ankle joint (བོལ་ཚིགས); calf; hollow of the knee; groove of the thigh; hip joint; above the kidneys; flank of the ribs; forearm; palm; neck (སྐེ་མགུལ); behind the ear and the jaw; nape and jaw; neck (སྐེ་མཇིང); palm; forearm; shoulder blade (དཔུང་སོག་སྟེང); flank of the ribs; above the kidneys; hip joint; groove of the thigh; hollow of the knee; above the knee; calf; ankle joint | the same, in the same words or near them (དཔྱི་མིག་ཚིགས / དཔྱི་ཚིགས, མཁལ་མའི་སྟེང / མཁལ་སྟེང) | agree |
| 4 | the waist (རྐེད་པ) | the knee (པུས་མོ) | differ |
| 10 | top of the shoulder (དཔུང་སྟེང) | shoulder blade (དཔུང་སོག) | near |
| 15 | shoulder blade (སོག་པ) | crown of the head, the whole body (སྤྱི་གཙུག་ལུས་སྤྱི) | differ |
| 29 | the shin (ངར་གདོང) | the instep (རྐང་བོལ) | differ |
| 30 | the whole body (མི་རྟའི་ལུས་པོ་བྱིངས) | the sole, the whole body | near |

WB's «བཞི་ལ་མི་ཡི་རྐེད་པ་ལ» and «བཅོ་ལྔ་མི་ཡི་སོག་པ་ལ» were read again on the scan (img. 311): the print has རྐེད་པ and སོག་པ. KL's three differences each restore the climb and descent that the rest of the list keeps (calf, knee, hollow of the knee; the crown at the top on the 15th; instep and sole before the 1st's big toe), where WB's printed words break it. Whether KL corrects WB from another witness or from the order is not said.

**KL's syllables are not WB's.** KL gives a syllable for dates 1–15 and 30 (ཨ ཨ ཨི ཨི ཨུ ཨུ ཨེ ཨེ རི རི ལི ལི ཨོ ཨོ ཨཾ, ཨཾ). WB's Phugpa list has none; WB's other list by date, after the Kālacakra (དུས་འཁོར, dus 'khor), has a syllable for every date and other places, read on the scan (p. 303, img. 311; the whole list, read again on the Ser thang reprint in O10, is in [lunar-dates.md](lunar-dates.md), *The text*, which reads the 3rd's and 5th's syllables as the vocalic «རྀ» and «ལྀ»):

> དེ་ལ་ཁྱད་པར་དུས་འཁོར་ལོའི། །དགོངས་པའི་བླ་གནས་འདི་ལྟར་རོ། །ཡར་ངོ་སྔོན་འགྲོའི་དབང་བྱས་ནས། །སྐྱེས་པའི་གཡོན་དང་བུད་མེད་གཡས།། དེ་ཡང་ཚེས་གཅིག་རྐང་པ་ཡི། །སོར་ཚིགས་དང་པོར་ཡི་གེ་ཨཿ། །གཉིས་པར་ཨི་དང་གསུམ་པར་རི། །བཞི་པར་ལོང་བུར་ཨུ་ཡིག་གཟུགས། །ལྔ་པར་པུས་མོར་ལི་ཡིག་གནས། …

The 1st at the first joint of the toe, «ཨཿ»; the 2nd «ཨི» and the 3rd «རི» at the next joints; the 4th at the ankle, «ཨུ»; the 5th at the knee, «ལི» (the scan has རི and ལི, as MITRA reads; Yigdzin-1 has རོ and ལ). By the machine reading here (read on the scan whole in [lunar-dates.md](lunar-dates.md), O10, 2026-10-13): the 6th at the hip, then the fingers' joints from the 7th, the elbow (11th), the shoulder (12th), the throat (13th), the forehead (15th), the crown «ཨཾ» at the junction of the 15th and 16th, and down again to the sole «ཨཿ» at the junction of the 30th and the 1st; each date its own syllable. KL's syllables instead go in pairs, one to two dates (ཨ ཨ ཨི ཨི ཨུ ཨུ ཨེ ཨེ རི རི ལི ལི ཨོ ཨོ ཨཾ), and sit on the Phugpa places. So KL joins the Phugpa places to a syllable sequence that is in neither of WB's lists.

**The la's course by hour (KL pp. 202–203) is WB's third list, vol. 2, p. 304 (img. 312), the course "within any day"**, after the *lho gter* list (lunar-dates.md summarises it; read here on the scan):

> ནམ་ལངས་རྐང་གཉིས་ཉི་ཤར་མཇིང་། །དྲོས་ཆུང་ལྟག་ཁུང་དྲོས་ཆེན་མཆུ། །ཉི་ཕྱེད་བྲང་གཞུང་ཕྱེད་ཡོལ་ལྟེ། །མྱུར་ཁ་སྙིང་ག་མྱུར་ཆུང་རྒྱབ། །ཉི་ནུབ་རྐང་པ་གཉིས་ལ་གནས། །ས་སྲོས་ནས་ནི་ནམ་ལངས་བར། །ལྟེ་བ་ཡན་ཆད་ཁྱབ་པར་གནས།

| Time (WB's words) | WB p. 304 | KL p. 202 (its words) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| daybreak (ནམ་ལངས) | both feet (རྐང་གཉིས) | «ལྒང་བ» |
| sunrise (ཉི་ཤར) | the neck (མཇིང) | the neck (མཇིང་བ) |
| early morning (དྲོས་ཆུང) | the hollow of the nape (ལྟག་ཁུང) | when the sun warms: the lips and the nape |
| late morning (དྲོས་ཆེན) | the lips (མཆུ) | (the same) |
| noon (ཉི་ཕྱེད) | the middle of the chest (བྲང་གཞུང) | the ribs of the chest |
| early afternoon (ཕྱེད་ཡོལ) | the navel (ལྟེ) | the belly (ལྟོ་བ) |
| late afternoon (མྱུར་ཁ) | the heart (སྙིང་ག) | when the sun is past: the heart |
| towards evening (མྱུར་ཆུང) | the back (རྒྱབ) | at dusk: the small of the back |
| sunset (ཉི་ནུབ) | both feet | — |
| dusk to daybreak (ས་སྲོས་ནས་ནམ་ལངས) | spread above the navel (ལྟེ་བ་ཡན་ཆད་ཁྱབ) | evening to daybreak: the whole body |

The order is the same; KL merges WB's two morning times and leaves out the feet at sunset. At daybreak WB has «རྐང་གཉིས», both feet, where KL prints «ལྒང་བ» (open question 60): read with WB, KL's word stands where the feet stand, and «ལྒང་བ» for «རྐང་པ», foot, would be a slip of sound; WB does not settle which KL meant. WB's other two lists by hour, the first (dawn the lips, sunrise inside the mouth …) and the *lho gter* list (a place and an hour for each date), are not KL's.

**The burning dates (KL p. 256): Friday's are the 2nd and the 17th in WB**, in both of its witnesses, the rule of vol. 1, p. 177 («པ་སངས་གཉིས་དང་བཅུ་བདུན་ཏེ») and the number words of vol. 2, p. 351 («འཁྲིག་པ», the pair), both read on the scan ([burning-dates.md](burning-dates.md)). Every other weekday's pair in KL is WB's too. So the scan of KL, ༢, agrees with WB, and BDRC's etext ༣ does not (open question 61).

**KL's six dates for moxa** («ཚེས། ༡། ༦། ༡༥། ༡༨། ༢༢། ༣༠», p. 256, "in agreement with the Tibetan reckoning") are not a list in WB. WB's lunar dates name bloodletting and moxa (གཏར་སྲེག, gtar sreg) by date as follows ([lunar-dates.md](lunar-dates.md), the table, read on the scan): to be avoided on the 4th («ལམ་འདུམས་གཏར་བསྲེག་དམག་འདྲེན་བག», p. 298), the 29th and the 30th; bloodletting alone (གཏར་ཁ, gtar kha) avoided on the 8th; both good on the 11th («ས་ཁ་གཏར་སྲེག་ལམ་དྲག་བཟང», p. 299) and on the 18th («ཐོག་འབུབས་སྒོ་འཛུགས་ལམ་ཞུགས་པ། །གཏར་སྲེག་གཤིན་ལས་རྒྱལ་ས་བྱ།», p. 300, in the good list). Ch. 34's work of bloodletting, moxa and the rod (vol. 2, pp. 403–404, img. 411–412; [white-beryl-ch34.md](white-beryl-ch34.md), work 33) gathers the same dates, «ཚེས་གྲངས་བཞི་བརྒྱད་ཉེར་དགུ་དང་། །གནམ་གང་རྣམས་ངན་བཅུ་གཅིག་དང་། །བཅོ་བརྒྱད་ལ་ནི་བཟང», the 4th, 8th, 29th and 30th bad, the 11th and 18th good. Only the 30th is in both lists; KL avoids the 18th, which WB counts good twice. Three of KL's six, the 15th, 22nd and 30th, are WB's perilous days (p. 302, with the 8th; [lunar-dates.md](lunar-dates.md), *The four dangerous times*), when falling ill is dangerous; the 1st, 15th and 30th touch the two junctions, of the 30th and 1st and of the 15th and 16th, that WB's Kālacakra list calls most perilous for the la («འདི་དང་ཨཾ་གི་དུས་ཀྱི་ཚེ། །བྱང་ཆུབ་སེམས་ཀྱིས་མགོ་ལུས་ཀུན། །ཁྱབ་ཕྱིར་ཤིན་ཏུ་གཉན་པ་སྟེ།», p. 303). The 6th and 18th have no such place in WB.

### Illness by lunar date (vol. 2, pp. 297–302, img. 305–310)

Each date's verse, after the birth, says what falling ill on it means: the cause the illness "answers" (ལན, lan), the spirits that harm, the signs, and the rite. [lunar-dates.md](lunar-dates.md) summarises these lines; here they are in full, read on the scan (Yigdzin-1 and MITRA agree on 99% of these pages; their flags here, «བཅོས» against «བཙས», «གཅོང» against «གཙང», «མྱང» against «མྱུང», read on the scan). Only dates 1–8 have lines of their own:

| Date | The illness line (p., img.) | In English |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | «ནད་ཐེབས་ཐབ་གཞོབ་འཕྲུ་རློག་དང་། །ཤིང་བཅད་སྔ་ཕྱིའི་འགྲུལ་གྱིས་རྐྱེན། །སྟོད་གཟེར་མིག་ནད་ཁོང་སྟོང་དང་། །གྲེ་མོ་ཀླུ་བཙན་གནོད་པ་ཡིན། །བོང་རྟ་བཙན་མདས་ཕྱུགས་སྲི་དགར།» (p. 297, img. 305) | The conditions are a defiled hearth (ཐབ་གཞོབ, thab gzhob), an act not identified (འཕྲུ་རློག, 'phru rlog), felled trees and journeys early and late; pain in the upper body, eye illness and an empty feeling inside; the female spirits (གྲེ་མོ, gre mo) and the nāga-*btsan* (ཀླུ་བཙན, klu btsan), nāgas joined with the fierce *btsan* spirits (བཙན, btsan), harm. The last line names the rites: a donkey and a horse (or goat, as ch. 25 has it, below), the *btsan*'s thread-cross (མདོས, mdos; printed «མདས»), and the livestock's harmful spirits of the dead (སྲི, sri) driven off («དགར», as «བཀར»); read so, not certain. |
| 2 | «ནད་བཏབ་ན། །འཁོན་དང་གཤིན་ཟས་མི་གཙང་ལན། །ལྷ་གདོན་གྲེ་བཙན་ས་བདག་གནོད། །ཕོ་མཆིན་མི་བདེ་[སྣོད]་གཞི་དགང་། །གཟེར་ནད་སྐྲངས་འབུར་ཤུ་ནད་འོང་། །མ་མོ་མཆོད་ཅིང་བསྐང་བཤགས་བྱ།» (pp. 297–298, img. 305–306) | It answers a grudge, food of the dead and impurity; harm from the gods as harmful spirits (ལྷ་གདོན, lha gdon), the *gre btsan* (གྲེ་བཙན, gre btsan; not identified further) and the earth lords (ས་བདག, sa bdag); stomach and liver uneasy, (the hollow organs?) filled; stabbing pains, swellings, lumps and an illness «ཤུ་ནད» (shu nad; ch. 25's reading of the same day has ཤུ་ཐོར, pustules) come. Worship the *ma mo* goddesses (མ་མོ, ma mo) and make the fulfilment and confession. The syllable before གཞི could be སྟོད (the upper body) on the scan. |
| 3 | «ནད་བཏབ་ཐབ་གཞོབ་བཅོས་བསྲེག་ལན། །ལྷ་གདོན་རྒྱལ་པོ་གྲེ་བོ་གནོད། །བསངས་ཁྲུས་ཆུ་གཏོར་རྒྱལ་མདོས་གཏོང་། །ཤེད་ཆུང་ཁོང་གཅོང་མཁལ་མ་ན། །རྡུལ་ཡོད་ཁ་སྐམ་བྱ་[མྱང]་བྱེད། །ཤི་བཟང་» (p. 298, img. 306) | It answers the hearth, handled and burnt; harm from the gods as spirits, the king spirits (རྒྱལ་པོ, rgyal po) and the male spirits (གྲེ་བོ, gre bo). Make the smoke offering, the ablution, the water offering and send a *rgyal po* thread-cross (རྒྱལ་མདོས, rgyal mdos). Weakness, a sickness within, pain in the kidneys; dust, a dry mouth (the last words not understood). A death is good. |
| 4 | «ནད་བཏབ་མནའ་ཡུགས་ཐབ་ཤོར་ལན། །རྒྱལ་འགོང་གཤིན་དང་ས་བདག་གནོད། །རྒྱལ་མདོས་ཀླུ་ཡས་གཟའ་མདོས་གཏོང་།» (p. 298, img. 306) | It answers an oath, a house of mourning and a hearth spoilt; the king-demons (རྒྱལ་འགོང, rgyal 'gong), the dead and the earth lords harm. Send the *rgyal po*'s and the planets' thread-crosses and a nāga offering not identified (ཀླུ་ཡས, klu yas). |
| 5 | «ནད་བཏབ་ཡུགས་སའི་ཟས་ཀྱིས་ལན། །བྲང་རྒྱབ་ཚ་ཞིང་གྲེ་བ་བསྐམས། །མོ་འདྲེ་ཆགས་འདྲེ་ས་བདག་གནོད། །ཁྲུས་དང་གཉན་མདོས་ས་བདག་གི། །ཤ་རུ་མདོས་སོགས་བཏང་བར་བྱ།» (p. 298, img. 306) | It answers food from a house of mourning; chest and back hot, the throat dry; the female demons, the demons of lust and the earth lords harm. Make an ablution and send the thread-cross of the *gnyan* spirits (གཉན, gnyan) and the earth lords' thread-cross not identified (ཤ་རུ་མདོས, sha ru mdos). |
| 6 | «ཤི་ངན་སྐྱེས་བསྟུད་ནད་འགྲུལ་ལན། །གྲེ་བཙན་བྱད་ཁ་སྲུང་མ་གནོད། །བཙན་མདོས་ཁྲོམ་མདོས་དགྲ་ལྷ་མཆོད། །གློ་གཟེར་སྨྱོ་འབོག་ཕོ་ལོག་རིམས།» (p. 298, img. 306) | Illness answers a journey; the *gre btsan*, curses (བྱད་ཁ, byad kha) and the protectors harm. Send the *btsan*'s and the crowd's thread-crosses (ཁྲོམ་མདོས, khrom mdos), worship the war god (དགྲ་ལྷ, dgra lha). Stabbing pain in the lungs, madness and fainting, vomiting, fever (རིམས, rims). |
| 7 | «ནད་བཏབ་ཕྱོགས་ངན་ཤ་མར་ཟས། །ནུ་མཆིན་རྩིབ་གཟེར་ཀླུ་གྲ་གནོད། །སྤྱི་མདོས་ཀླུ་མཆོད་གྲེ་མོ་བརྡུང་། །ཤི་ན་འཆི་རྗེས་བཟང་བ་ཡིན།» (pp. 298–299, img. 306–307) | It answers a bad direction and food of meat and butter; pains in the breasts, the liver and the ribs; the nāgas and a spirit not identified (ཀླུ་གྲ, klu gra) harm. Send the general thread-cross, worship the nāgas, strike the *gre mo*. A death is good for those after. |
| 8 | «གང་སྐྱེས་གསོ་དཀའ་ནད་བཏབ་ན། །མིག་ནད་མཁལ་རྐེད་སྙིང་རླུང་ལྡང་། །ལྷ་ཁང་སྲུང་མ་རྒྱལ་པོ་གནོད། །རྒྱལ་མདོས་དམ་སྲི་དགར་ཤིང་ན།» (p. 299, img. 307) | Eye illness, the kidneys and the waist, and the heart-wind (སྙིང་རླུང, snying rlung) rises; the temple's protectors and the *rgyal po* harm. A *rgyal po* thread-cross, and the oath-breaking harmful spirits of the dead (དམ་སྲི, dam sri) to be driven out (the last words read so, not understood). |

**Dates 9–30 take one of these eight in turn.** The verses say «X མཚུངས», "the same as X": the 9th as the 1st, the 10th as the 2nd, … the 16th as the 8th, the 17th as the 1st again, the 24th as the 8th, the 25th as the 1st, the 30th as the 6th ([lunar-dates.md](lunar-dates.md), the last column, read on the scan). So date *d* falls ill as date ((*d* − 1) mod 8) + 1, an eight-day round over the month; ch. 33 states the matches one by one and does not name the round.

**The round is the eight goddesses'.** Ch. 25 (vol. 1, pp. 449–450, img. 459–460; quoted, read on the scan, in [lunar-day-signs.md](lunar-day-signs.md)) counts eight goddesses through the month, 'Od 'bar ma on the 1st, 9th, 17th and 25th, Bstan ma on the 2nd, 10th, 18th and 26th, and so round, and gives each her cause, her spirits, her rites and her illness. Ch. 33's eight date lines are the same readings, shorter and in nearly the same words: the 1st's hearth, felled trees and journeys, nāga-*btsan* and *gre mo*, and «བོང་རྟ་བཙན་མདས» where ch. 25 has «བོང་ར་བཙན་མདོས» (a donkey and goat with the *btsan*'s thread-cross: ch. 33's «མདས» looks like a slip for «མདོས»); the 2nd's grudge and impurity, the same spirits, «ཕོ་མཆིན་མི་བདེ» and the stabbing pains and swellings; the 3rd's hearth, kidneys and dust; the 4th's *rgyal 'gong*, the dead and earth lords; the 5th's food of a house of mourning; the 6th's contagion, madness and fainting; the 7th's food from a bad direction, liver and ribs, nāgas and *gre mo*; the 8th's kidneys and waist, eyes and heart-wind, and the *dam sri* driven off («བཀར», ch. 25; ch. 33 prints «དགར»). So WB's illness by lunar date is one reading in two places, ch. 25 counting it as the goddesses' round, ch. 33 by the date.

**The four perilous days** (p. 302, img. 310, [lunar-dates.md](lunar-dates.md)): the 8th, 15th, 22nd and 30th, «འདི་དུས་ནད་ཐེབས་འདུ་ལོང་གི། །བྱ་བ་རྔམ་དྲག་ཆེ་རྣམས་ངན།», falling ill on them is bad, and gatherings and any great or fierce work.

### Illness by weekday (vol. 2, pp. 308–312, img. 316–320)

Each weekday's verse, after the rain and the portents, says what falling ill on it means, then a theft, a remedy and war; [weekdays.md](weekdays.md) summarises these. The illness lines, Yigdzin-1 with MITRA as witness (2026-10-12; 93–95% of each page's syllables agree), every flag in these lines (Sunday's ཆུ, Monday's བཅོས, Tuesday's བཤས, གཞོབ, ཆོག, Wednesday's ཆོག, Saturday's རློག, བཅོས) and every line read on the scan:

| Weekday (p., img.) | The illness line | In English |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Sunday (p. 308, img. 316) | «ནད་ཀྱིས་བཏབ་ན་དཀར་གྱི་ཕྱིར། །འབྲངས་པའི་འདུད་བཙན་གྲེ་བོ་གནོད། །བཙན་མདོས་བདུད་མདོས་ཀླུ་བརྒྱད་དང་། །ཆུ་གཏོར་ལོ་གྲངས་བྱིས་སྟོན་བྱ། །ངར་གླུད་དྲི་ཅན་ནང་གོས་དྲིལ། །ས་སྲོས་འདྲེ་ལྔ་ཕྱོགས་སུ་སྐྱེལ།» | Falling ill, the demon-*btsan* (printed «འདུད», for བདུད, bdud, demon) and the male spirits harm, who followed after white (food or goods, not said); the *btsan*'s and the demons' thread-crosses, the eight nāgas, a water offering and a feast for as many children as the sick one's years; a ransom (གླུད, glud) wrapped in a fragrant undergarment, sent at dusk towards the five *'dre* (འདྲེ, 'dre, evil spirits). |
| Monday (p. 309, img. 317) | «ནད་ཀྱིས་བཏབ་ན་ཡུགས་སའི་ཕྱིར། །འབྲངས་པའི་ཀླུ་སྲན་གཤིན་འདྲེ་གནོད། །ཆུ་གཏོར་མ་མོའི་མདོས་ཀྱིས་བཅོས། །ལུག་གྲོད་ཆུ་ཕོར་ལོ་གྲངས་བླུགས། །ཉ་སྦྲུལ་གཟུགས་དང་གླུད་དྲོ་ཅན། །བླུགས་ལ་ས་སྲོས་ཆུ་འཐབ་བསྐྱལ།» | The nāga-ogres (printed «ཀླུ་སྲན», read as ཀླུ་སྲིན, klu srin, as Thursday's line has it) and the spirits of the dead harm, who followed after a house of mourning; treat with a water offering and the *ma mo*'s thread-cross; in a sheep's stomach and a water bowl pour as many measures as the years, with figures of fish and snakes and a ransom «དྲོ་ཅན» (not understood), and send them at dusk to where waters meet. |
| Tuesday (p. 309, img. 317) | «ནད་བཏབ་དམར་དང་བཤས་ཀྱིས་ལེན། །ཐབ་གཞོབ་མི་གཙང་ཕྱོགས་ངན་པའི། །དམར་ཕྱིར་བཙན་དང་གྲེ་བོ་གནོད། །གཟུངས་ཆོག་ལྷ་གསོལ་བཙན་ཆོག་བྱ།» | Illness comes from red (flesh and blood) and slaughter; the *btsan* and the male spirits harm, who followed the red of a defiled hearth, impurity and a bad direction; dhāraṇī rites, offerings to the gods and the *btsan*'s rite. |
| Wednesday (p. 310, img. 318) | «ནད་བཏབ་བན་བོན་བྱད་ཁའམ། །བུ་སྲིང་ཕྱིར་འབྲངས་ཀླུ་གནོད་པས། །གྲིབ་སེལ་ཀླུ་ཆོག་བྱས་ན་བཟང་།» | Illness from the curse of a monk or a Bonpo, or the nāgas who followed after a son or a sister harm; clearing the defilement (གྲིབ, grib) and a nāga rite are good. Born on it, a boy is sickly («ནད་བུ་ཅན»). |
| Thursday (p. 311, img. 319) | «ནད་བཏབ་མཚན་མོའི་འགྲུལ་གྱི་ཕྱིར། །འབྲངས་པའི་ཀླུ་སྲིན་རྒྱལ་པོ་གནོད། །ཟོར་མདོས་ཀླུ་བརྒྱད་བྱས་ན་ཕན།» | The nāga-ogres and the king spirits harm, who followed after a journey by night; the *zor* thread-cross (ཟོར, zor, a ritual missile) and the eight nāgas help. Falling ill (ནད་ཐེབས) is in its bad list too. |
| Friday (p. 311, img. 319) | «ནད་བཏབ་ན། །ཚོང་ཕྱིར་འབྲངས་པའི་རྒྱལ་བཙན་གནོད། །ཀློག་འདོན་བསངས་སེལ་བཙན་ཆོག་བྱ།» | The king-*btsan* who followed after trade harm; reading scripture, fumigation and clearing, the *btsan*'s rite. Falling ill is in its bad list; a girl born on it is sickly. |
| Saturday (p. 312, img. 320) | «ནད་ཀྱིས་བཏབ་ན་གཤིན་རྗེ་དང་། །ས་བདག་ཐེའུ་བྲང་མ་མོ་གནོད། །དུས་ཀྱི་ཆོ་ག་ཆག་པ་དང་། །འཕྲུ་རློག་འཁུར་བ་གསུར་གྱིས་ལན། །གཟུངས་འདོན་ཀླུ་བརྒྱད་ས་བདག་བཅོས།» | The lord of death (གཤིན་རྗེ, gshin rje), the earth lords, the *the'u rang* and the *ma mo* harm; it answers a seasonal rite broken, *'phru rlog*, carrying, and burnt offerings (གསུར, gsur); recite dhāraṇīs, treat the eight nāgas and the earth lords. |

On the scan at 3×, Monday's «ཀླུ་སྲན» and «དྲོ» are as both readers have them; nāga-ogres (ཀླུ་སྲིན) is a reading, not the print.

### The day animals (vol. 2, pp. 356–359, img. 364–367)

The verses are quoted and read on the scan in [day-animals.md](day-animals.md); their lines on health, gathered:

| dragon (p. 357) | bloodletting and moxa («གཏར་ག་མེ་བཙའ») avoided |
| snake (p. 357) | examining or treating with medicine («སྨན་དཔྱད») and bloodletting and moxa («གཏར་སྲེག») avoided; moving the sick to another place («ནད་པ་ཕྱོགས་འདོན») good |

| dog (p. 358) | washing the head avoided, for it brings talk and grief; binding the mouths of the *bse rag* spirits (བསེ་རགས, bse rags) and medicine («ཁ་བཅིངས་སྨན») good |
| mouse (p. 358) | moxa (མེ་བཙའ, me btsa') in the bad list, with words left as doubtful («རྐེད་མན་ཐག་རིངས་དོན»); caring for children («བྱིས་པ་གསོ») good |
